Disperon QED
Yizhou Fang ​, Sophie Kollatzsch ​, Marco Rocco ​, Adrian Signer ​, Yannick Ulrich ​, Max Zoller ​

Abstract
We present disperon QED, a method to deal with data input in loop processes in Monte Carlo codes. It relies on dispersion relations, automated tools such as OpenLoops , effective field theory methods and a threshold subtraction. We motivate this method and apply it to the process e ​ e → π ​ π ee\to\pi\pi in McMule to deal with hadronic vacuum polarisation insertions in two-loop contributions as well as the vector form factor of the pion within the form-factor sQED approximation. The generality of this method for more complicated processes is emphasised.



1

Introduction

Low-energy scattering processes play a vital role in addressing many
current fundamental questions in particle physics. There is a rich
experimental program for electron-positron scattering into hadronic or
leptonic final states and for lepton-proton scattering at energies well below the electroweak scale, to name two of
the most active fields. In both cases, the situation is currently
rather confusing. If
e^{+}\,e^{-}
scattering is used to extract the
contribution of the
HVP
(
HVP
) to the
anomalous magnetic moment of the muon
[
1
]
, there
is a considerable tension between various experimental
results. Furthermore, most of them seem to be in conflict with recent
lattice calculations
[
2
,
3
,
4
]
. If lepton-proton scattering is used to
extract the proton form factor at various values of the momentum
transfer, there are also tensions between different experiments and
methods used, see
[
5
]
for a review. Furthermore, most
scattering experiments lead to a proton radius that is in conflict
with the most precise spectroscopy result of muonic
hydrogen
[
6
]
.

Precise Monte Carlo tools for such processes are crucial to
investigate possible reasons for these discrepancies. Triggered by
these observations, renewed efforts and initiatives have been started
to improve these tools
[
7
,
5
]
and exploit the
computational advances of recent years. Regarding pure QED corrections
with pointlike constituents, there has indeed been substantial
progress. Currently, leptonic
2\to{2}
processes have been computed at

NNLO
(
NNLO
) in QED
[
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
]
as well as in
all-order resummation improved
NLO
(
NLO
) calculations in a fully differential way
[
16
,
17
,
18
,
19
]
. Electroweak
NLO
corrections are also available and are combined with QED corrections into Monte Carlo codes
[
20
,
21
,
22
,
23
]
. For
2\to{3}
processes, progress towards
the same perturbative order is ongoing
[
24
,
25
,
26
,
27
]
and likely to lead to concrete result in the
near future. Improved theory for
2\to{3}
processes is urgently needed, since radiative processes with an
additional photon in the final state play a decisive role in the
experimental extraction of the
HVP
.

With increasing precision in perturbative calculations for low-energy processes, the question of
how to include non-perturbative contributions becomes more
urgent. Indeed, all processes mentioned above will at some point
depend on insertions of
HVP
or hadronic light-by-light contributions. For processes with external hadrons
h
, additional hadronic matrix elements such as the Compton tensor
\gamma^{*}\,\gamma^{*}\to h\,h
need to be embedded into amplitudes. The standard procedure to do this is to decompose the hadronic matrix elements into Lorentz structures and determine their scalar coefficients numerically from data
[
28
,
29
,
30
]
. Once the coefficients are known, the question arises how to combine the hadronic part with the remaining amplitude.
As long as the hadronic matrix elements only enter tree-level amplitudes, they
can be included through simple multiplications.
However, if they appear within loops, the momentum dependence of the matrix elements results in a much more complicated situation. The resulting loop integrations cannot be done with standard tools.

Here we focus on insertions of
HVP
and form factors into loop calculations. Regarding the latter, it has been shown that using the
sQED
(
sQED
) vertex multiplied by the
VFF
(
VFF
) for photon-pion interactions corresponds to the inclusion of the pion-pole terms in the pion Compton tensor
[
29
]
.
While this correspondence does not hold in the case of protons or with more than two photons attached to the pions, form-factor improved QED is a meaningful first step towards a more reliable description of structure-dependent corrections in low-energy processes with external hadrons
[
31
,
32
,
7
,
33
]
.

A non-trivial inclusion of
HVP
effects for scattering processes
appears only at
NNLO
where it is similar to the
HVP
corrections to an anomalous magnetic moment.
Recent examples include the evaluation of the HVP insertions
for muon-electron scattering at
NNLO
through the
dispersive
[
34
]
or the hyperspherical
approach
[
35
]
. Based on these techniques, several fully
differential
NNLO
calculations have been carried out with the
inclusion of
HVP
effects
[
12
,
14
,
13
]
. For the anomalous
magnetic moment, even higher orders have been
computed
[
36
]
. The applied techniques do not rely on
a particular functional form of the
HVP
. Thus, they can be easily
adapted if a more precise non-perturbative input becomes available.

The situation regarding inclusion of form factors in loop
contributions to fully differential observables is less advanced. So
far, problems of this nature have been solved on a case by case basis.
Contributions with a pion
VFF
within a loop for

ee\to\pi\pi
have been computed in an
analytic way at one loop through the
GVMD
(
GVMD
)
[
37
,
38
]
. Similarly, the inclusion of the proton
form factor in
TPE
(
TPE
) for elastic lepton-proton scattering
typically relies on soft-photon approximations
[
31
,
32
,
39
,
40
]
or particular analytic representations such as a
dipole form factor
[
41
,
15
]
. However, these methods become increasingly tedious for more complicated final states. Furthermore, they often are not amenable to generic forms of the hadronic input, but rather rely on particular parametrisations thereof.
TPE
contributions have also been evaluated without model dependence in an
EFT
(
EFT
) framework
[
42
,
43
,
44
]
or through dispersion relations, e.g.
[
45
,
46
,
47
]
. Reviews focusing on TPE in lepton-proton scattering are provided in
[
48
,
49
,
50
,
51
]
.

In order to deal with a generic numerical input, we need a recipe that brings the dependence on the loop momentum
k
into a form that is compatible with standard loop calculations.
The most common approach is to express the numerical function
F(k^{2})
through a (once-subtracted) dispersion relation

\displaystyle\frac{F(k^{2})}{k^{2}}=\frac{F(0)}{k^{2}}-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{s_{\text{thr}}}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}{s_{1}}}{{s_{1}}}\frac{\mathrm{Im}\,F({s_{1}})}{k^{2}-{s_{1}}}\,,

(1)

solve the loop integral analytically and subsequently integrate numerically over the dispersion parameter
[
52
]
. At the price of an additional integration over the
dispersive parameter
{s_{1}}
starting from the pair-production threshold
s_{\text{thr}}
, this allows to reduce the expressions to
standard loop integrals.

In the context of
VFF
insertions for the process

ee\to\pi\pi
, such a procedure
was used in
[
53
]
to compute the forward-backward asymmetry. The obtained results agree well with the
GVMD

calculation
[
38
]
and with data
[
54
,
55
]
. These
methods were also combined with a parton shower in the
Monte Carlo code BabaYaga@NLO
[
18
]
. The results clearly
show that an external multiplication of the box diagrams with a form
factor
[
56
,
57
,
58
]
-- a procedure dubbed as
FxsQED
(
FxsQED
)
in
[
7
]
-- gives poor agreement with experimental
data, whereas the
FsQED
(
FsQED
) approach
[
59
,
29
,
53
]
based on the dispersion relation (
1
) with
VFF
in the loop leads to
substantial improvements.

In view of the importance of radiative processes such as

ee\to\pi\pi\gamma
, it is desirable to extend the
FsQED

approach to
2\to{3}
or even more complicated processes. So far,
this process has only been considered in the
FxsQED

approach
[
60
,
57
,
61
]
.
While
FsQED
will not capture all
non-perturbative effects, it constitutes a major improvement, in
particular for the usually dominant
contributions where the additional photon is emitted from the electron line
[
7
]
.
However, extending the previous
calculations to situations with more external legs becomes
increasingly tedious.

The purpose of this article is to propose a technical procedure that facilitates the combination of the dispersive approach with the use of automated tools for loop calculations. We call this method
disperon QED
. While the proposed
methods are universal, for the presentation of concrete results we
focus on the process
ee\to\pi\pi
. We consider the inclusion of the
VFF

in loops at NLO and the
HVP
effects restricted to
NNLO

ISC
(
ISC
), where photons are only exchanged among initial-state electrons.
These effects are implemented in the
McMule

framework
[
9
,
62
]
, a
NNLO
Monte Carlo tool for QED processes that is based on
FKSl
(
FKSl
) subtraction
[
63
]
and
NTS
stabilisation
[
11
]
.
The code and results obtained with it are publicly available

https://mule-tools.gitlab.io/

Disperon QED relies on the fact that the dispersive approach transforms a photon propagator into a propagator for a massive vector boson that we dub
disperon
. Its mass
is given by the dispersive parameter and needs to be integrated
over. For moderate values of the mass, the corresponding loop diagrams
will be evaluated with
OpenLoops

[
64
,
65
]
. For very large values of
this mass, we improve numerical stability and speed by turning to
a description inspired by
EFT

considerations. This custom-made
EFT
,
DET
(
DET
), is obtained by integrating out the heavy
disperon and keeping terms up to a certain order in the inverse mass. As
we will show, combining these techniques with a threshold subtraction,
we can address the inclusion of
VFF
in the loop in an efficient
manner. In addition, it allows us to not only include a single
HVP

insertion, but even use a resummed HVP within a loop. While
the effect of resummation is small for the
ISC

considered here, the resummation can have substantial effect in the

s
-channel near resonances.

We start in Section
2
with the basic idea of the dispersive
approach and how it relates directly to disperon QED.
In Section
2.1
we address
HVP
insertions, followed in Section
2.2

by
VFF
insertions, to achieve a
FsQED
description. The modifications
that are required in
OpenLoops
to deal with a disperon are described
in Section
3
. Section
4
is devoted to the EFT considerations
that are required to deal with the large-disperon-mass region in the
dispersive integral. A final ingredient needed is how to deal with
threshold singularities. This will be done in Section
5
. Then we
are ready to show phenomenological results for the process

ee\to\pi\pi
in Section
6
. Apart from presenting our
conclusions in Section
7
, we also give an outlook for future
applications to more complicated processes. Some technical details are
delegated to several appendices.

2

Numerical input functions in loop integrals

When calculating higher-order corrections that at any point involve composite particles such as hadrons, we need to solve loop integrals that include functions depending on the loop momentum
k
but are presumed to be only known numerically.
The way out is to use a dispersive representation of these functions.
Here, we will consider the
HVP
function
\Pi_{h}
or the pion
VFF

F_{\pi}^{V}
as examples, though other numerical input functions can be treated the same way. We will use the once-subtracted dispersive representation (
1
) with
s_{\text{thr}}={4m_{\pi}^{2}}
. The interpretation of
k^{2}=k^{2}+i\delta
in (
1
) is always understood, even if often suppressed in the notation.

We stress that the disperon QED method is not limited to those scenarios but is applicable wherever a dispersion theorem like (
1
) exists, with a function
F(k^{2})
that fulfils the requirements set out in Appendix
A
. For the sake of completeness a proof of (
1
) is also sketched in Appendix
A
.
In the following, we will discuss the use of (
1
) for (hadronic)
VP
(
VP
) insertions and the calculation of one-loop corrections in
FsQED
.
As an example, we will consider the process
ee\to\pi\pi
but the same technology can be applied to more complicated processes such as
ee\to\pi\pi+n\gamma
.
Moreover, it enables us to compute the (hadronic)
VP
contribution to any QED process at two loop.

2.1

HVP with a disperon

Consider the simple case of a single
VP
insertion. The photon propagator gets modified

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k^{2}}\to\frac{\Pi(k^{2})}{k^{2}}

(2)

for a suitable routing where the momentum flowing through the
VP
is exactly
k
.

\Pi
is renormalised on-shell, i.e.
\Pi(0)=0
.
In the case of hadrons (
\Pi_{h}
) it is related to the measurable
R
-ratio as

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\,\Pi_{h}(s)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha}{3}R(s)=-\frac{s}{4\pi\alpha}\sigma(e^{+}e^{-}\to{\rm hadrons})\,,

(3)

where
\alpha
is the QED coupling.
Hence, we cannot solve the integral containing (
2
) directly.
Instead, we write, e.g.
[
66
]

\displaystyle\frac{\Pi_{h}(k^{2})}{k^{2}}=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{4m_{\pi}^{2}}}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}{s_{1}}}{{s_{1}}}\frac{\mathrm{Im}\,\Pi_{h}({s_{1}})}{k^{2}-{s_{1}}+i\delta}\,,

(4)

using (
1
) with
\Pi(0)=0
.
The dispersion integral runs from threshold, i.e.
s_{\text{thr}}={4m_{\pi}^{2}}
in the case of
HVP
, to infinity.
The important point is that the
k
dependence of the r.h.s. of (
4
) is now of the form of a propagator which allows us to use standard loop calculus.
The new propagator
1/(k^{2}-{s_{1}})
looks like the propagator of a massive photon, a disperon, whose mass
\sqrt{{s_{1}}}
corresponds to the dispersion parameter.
Since the
HVP
insertion takes place on any photon propagator, we can perform the calculation in a theory that has the disperon as a particle in addition to the photon.
The disperon has the exact same coupling structure as the normal photon and we can use standard loop technology to generate and evaluate diagrams in disperon QED without having to worry that it originated from a dispersive integral.
We note that the disperon has no effect on the underlying gauge symmetry of QED.
Once the loop calculation is completed, we need only to remember to multiply with
\mathrm{Im}\,\Pi_{h}({s_{1}})
and accompanying prefactors, and numerically integrate over
{s_{1}}
.
As we will eventually implement this calculation in a Monte Carlo code, we will do this as part of the phase-space integration.

Let us now make the discussion above more concrete.
For the process under consideration,
ee\to\pi\pi
,
VP
contributions can only enter at
NNLO
since the
VP
insertion into a tree-level diagram is already covered by the
VFF
.
The ISC
VP
contribution is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{(2)}_{\rm VP}=\begin{gathered}\hbox to89.21pt{\vbox to60.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 30.37851pt\lower-30.37851pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{gathered}\supset\int[{\rm d}k]\frac{\Pi(k^{2})}{\big[k^{2}\big]\big[(k+p)^{2}-m_{e}^{2}\big]\big[(k-q)^{2}-m_{e}^{2}\big]}\,,

where
[{\rm d}k]
is using the Package-X
[
67
,
68
]
convention.
Other contributions, for example when the
VP
is inside a box, are not part of the
ISC
and hence beyond the approximation that is considered within this work.

Before we can use the dispersion relation, let us convince ourselves that the conditions discussed in Appendix
A
are fulfilled.
We do this by studying the perturbative case for a particle with mass
m\neq 0
at one loop

\displaystyle\frac{\Pi(k^{2})}{k^{2}}=\frac{\alpha}{18\pi k^{2}}\Bigg(6\beta^{2}+3(\beta^{2}-3)\beta\log\frac{\beta-1+i\delta}{\beta+1}-16\Bigg)\,,

(6)

where we have defined
\beta=\sqrt{1-4m^{2}/k^{2}}
.
This function has the necessary branch cut along the real axis from
k^{2}=4m^{2}
.
Near threshold (
k^{2}=4m^{2}
,
\beta\sim 0
), this becomes

\displaystyle\frac{\Pi(k^{2})}{k^{2}}\Bigg|_{k^{2}\sim 4m^{2}}=-\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\frac{2}{9m^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(\beta)\,,

(7)

which is sufficiently well-behaved.
For large
k^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\Pi(k^{2})}{k^{2}}\Bigg|_{k^{2}\sim\infty}=-\frac{\alpha}{9\pi k^{2}}\bigg(5+3\log\frac{-m^{2}}{k^{2}}\bigg)+\mathcal{O}(k^{-4})\,,

(8)

which falls off fast enough for the dispersion relation to be valid.

In the case of
\Pi_{h}
, the high-energy limit is given by an operator product expansion in perturbative QCD.
The leading contribution, ignoring all
\mathcal{O}{(\alpha_{s})}
terms, is given by the simple vacuum polarisation used in (
6
) multiplied with the appropriate prefactors.
Hence, the limit is well-behaved and reads

\displaystyle\frac{\Pi_{h}(k^{2})}{k^{2}}\Bigg|_{k^{2}\sim\infty}=\frac{\Pi(k^{2})}{k^{2}}\cdot N_{c}\sum_{f}Q_{f}^{2}\Bigg|_{k^{2}\sim\infty}\,.

(9)

At the threshold,
k^{2}={4m_{\pi}^{2}}
,
\Pi_{h}(k^{2})
is only accessible through data.
Because the first resonance, the
\rho/\omega
, is not close to the threshold (indeed, it is at
\approx(7-8)\times\,{4m_{\pi}^{2}}
), we can approximate
\Pi_{h}(k^{2})
as a linear function which satisfies the conditions in Appendix
A
.

We note that using the simple replacement (
2
) close to narrow resonances such as the
J/\psi
can be very delicate.
It is often customary to use Dyson resummation instead and write

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k^{2}}\to\frac{1}{k^{2}}\frac{1}{1-\sum_{i}\Pi_{i}(k^{2})}\equiv\frac{\Pi_{\rm res}(k^{2})}{k^{2}}\,,

(10)

where the sum is over both leptonic and hadronic
VP
. Since
\sum_{i}\Pi_{i}(k^{2})\neq{1}
for all values of
k^{2}
, this new
F(k^{2})
has the same analyticity properties and acceptable limiting behaviour

\displaystyle\frac{\Pi_{\rm res}(k^{2})}{k^{2}}\Bigg|_{k^{2}\sim 4m^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4m^{2}}\frac{1}{1+\frac{8\alpha}{9\pi}}+\mathcal{O}(\beta)\,,

(11)

\displaystyle\frac{\Pi_{\rm res}(k^{2})}{k^{2}}\Bigg|_{k^{2}\sim\infty}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k^{2}}\frac{1}{1+\frac{\alpha}{9\pi}\big(5+3\log\frac{-m^{2}}{k^{2}}\big)}+\mathcal{O}(k^{-4})\,.

(12)

We therefore conclude that
\Pi_{\rm res}
is an acceptable
F
but note that
\Pi_{\rm res}(0)=1
instead of
\Pi(0)=0
which means that the dispersion relation (
4
) would need to be modified.
To avoid this, we instead define
\Pi_{\rm res}^{\prime}=\Pi_{\rm res}-1
which also avoids mixing up the pure QED vertex correction at
NLO
(the
\Pi_{\rm res}(0)=1
term) and the
NNLO

VP
effects.
Therefore, we may now use the replacement (
4
) for (
2.1
) and arrive at

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{(2)}_{\rm VP}\supset\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{4m_{e}^{2}}^{\infty}{\rm d}{s_{1}}\ \frac{\mathrm{Im}\,\Pi({s_{1}})}{{s_{1}}}\int[{\rm d}k]\frac{1}{\big[k^{2}-{s_{1}}\big]\big[(k+p)^{2}-m_{e}^{2}\big]\big[(k-q)^{2}-m_{e}^{2}\big]}\,.

(13)

Note that in the case of the simple replacement (
2
), this procedure can also be used for only the hadronic (leptonic) contributions to the
VP
, where the lowest threshold is
{4m_{\pi}^{2}}
(
4m_{e}^{2}
) instead.
The general form (
13
) can be easily written using disperon QED

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{(2)}_{\rm VP}\supset\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{4m_{e}^{2}}^{\infty}{\rm d}{s_{1}}\frac{\mathrm{Im}\,\Pi({s_{1}})}{{s_{1}}}\,\,\begin{gathered}\hbox to89.21pt{\vbox to60.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 30.37851pt\lower-30.37851pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

where we have used a zigzag line to indicate the disperon.

We have thus reduced the inclusion of
VP
contributions to standard amplitudes with a new massive particle. For the computation of such amplitudes, we can use the full machinery available.

2.2

FsQED with a disperon

The method presented for the inclusion of the
HVP
can be adapted
to any hadronic matrix element depending on a single momentum. However, in more complicated situations we need to include
hadronic matrix elements with more than two external legs. In
particular, for the process
ee\to\pi\pi
at
NLO
the
hadronic matrix element
\gamma^{*}\,\gamma^{*}\,\pi\pi
(with
off-shell photons), i.e. the (doubly virtual) pion Compton tensor, is
needed.

Using the full Compton tensor as a building block for the process
ee\to\pi\pi
is trivial, as long as the
tensor does not appear in a loop. If it does, it is currently not
feasible to use the complete expression. The full expression for this matrix element has a kinematic
structure that is extremely difficult to reconcile with standard loop
calculations
[
69
]
.
Fortunately, as detailed in Appendix
B
, the
pole part of the pion Compton tensor corresponds to the
sQED

version, multiplied by the
VFF

F_{\pi}^{V}
that describes the interaction of an electromagnetic current with a pair of charged on-shell pions and is defined as

\displaystyle\langle\pi^{+}(p)\pi^{-}(q)|j_{em}^{\mu}(0)|0\rangle=(-ie)F^{V}_{\pi}\big((p+q)^{2}\big)(q^{\mu}-p^{\mu})=\begin{gathered}\hbox to93.48pt{\vbox to57.14pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 41.34425pt\lower-28.45216pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

where the blob corresponds to
F_{\pi}^{V}
.
Thus, the use of
FxsQED
for the Compton tensor is a good approximation, at least at low energies.

Once we add a loop integral -- e.g. by attaching an electron line to the two off-shell photons -- the
FxsQED
Compton tensor leads to a situation akin to the
HVP
case discussed in Section
2.1
where we have the
VFF
in a loop integral.
For the amplitude
ee\to\pi\pi
, using the
FxsQED
Compton tensor leads to a description called
FsQED
.
We generalise this notion and use
FsQED
as a technical definition for the calculation of
any amplitude with external pions, whereby we use
sQED
but
locally (i.e. also within loops) multiply the photon-pion interaction
with
VFF
. This has to be distinguished from a
FxsQED

approach, where the
VFF
is multiplied externally, i.e. after
the loop integration has been carried out. In this latter approach,
the momentum to be used in the
VFF
is ambiguous. Furthermore, as
shown in
[
38
,
53
,
18
]
,
FxsQED
does not give a satisfactory description of the forward-backward asymmetry of
ee\to\pi\pi
, that we will define later in (
67
).

In
[
7
]
, FsQED was defined as including the pole terms of the
hadronic matrix elements. For
ee\to\pi\pi
, this agrees with our technical
definition.
This is also the
case for

ee\to\pi\pi\gamma
at
NLO
if the additional photon emission is
from the electron line. However, the correspondence ceases to hold for

ee\to\pi\pi\gamma
one-loop contributions with additional photon emission from the pion line. As discussed in Appendix
B
, there is
no one-to-one correspondence between the
FxsQED
version of a

\pi\,\pi\,n\,\gamma
matrix element and its pole terms
for
n\geq 3
. Still, in these more complicated cases, the use
of
VFF
within loops is a meaningful first step towards a more
reliable description of processes with external pions
[
33
]
.

To summarise, we will
keep using the term
FsQED
for matrix elements evaluated with this
approach and in this section we will describe how to use disperon QED
methods to deal with the corresponding technical issues. In passing, we also note that using form
factors in loops for
e\,p\to{e}\,p
does not correspond to keeping
the pole terms in the hadronic matrix element

\gamma^{*}\,\gamma^{*}\,p\,p
. But the notion of
FsQED
as described here
can still be applied and constitutes a starting point for improving

TPE
contributions in electron-proton scattering.

Turning to the concrete example of
NLO
corrections to
ee\to\pi\pi
, we have
ISC
corrections

Those corrections are trivial w.r.t. the inclusion of the
VFF
and do not need to be discussed.

Further, we require so-called mixed contributions with two photons connecting the initial-state electrons with the final-state pions.
Our previous discussion of the Compton tensor allows us to approximate this as

where we include a
VFF
for each grey blob.
By bringing the
F_{\pi}^{V}
into the loop, we transition from an
FxsQED
description of
\gamma^{*}\gamma^{*}\to\pi\pi
to an
FsQED
description of
ee\to\pi\pi
.
It is possible to fit
F_{\pi}^{V}
to propagators to perform an analytic loop integration as done in the
GVMD
approach.
However, we will not rely on such a parameterisation of
F^{V}_{\pi}
.

FSC
(
FSC
) corrections, i.e. corrections only associated to the pions,
are not considered within this work.
As discussed in
[
7
]
,
sQED
may provide a reasonable approximation for those corrections in
ee\to\pi\pi

[
70
]
.
In
[
18
]
, these corrections were implemented for
ee\to\pi\pi
using
FsQED
and
GVMD
.
The effects are of a few percent relative to LO for observables that do not require additional photons, e.g.
{\rm d}\sigma/{\rm d}\theta
.

The
VFF
vanishes at high energies
[
71
]

\displaystyle\frac{F_{\pi}^{V}(k^{2})}{k^{2}}\Bigg|_{k^{2}\sim\infty}=\frac{1}{k^{2}}\frac{8\pi\alpha_{s}(k^{2})f^{2}_{\pi}}{k^{2}}\,,

(20)

where
f_{\pi}
is the pion decay constant and
\alpha_{s}
the strong coupling.
This allows us to decompose
F^{V}_{\pi}
dispersively as

\displaystyle\frac{F_{\pi}^{V}(k^{2})}{k^{2}}=\frac{1}{k^{2}}-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{4m_{\pi}^{2}}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}{s_{1}}}{{s_{1}}}\frac{\mathrm{Im}\,F_{\pi}^{V}({s_{1}})}{k^{2}-{s_{1}}}\,.

(21)

In contrast to (
4
) we now have
F_{\pi}^{V}(0)=1
.
When we use this to calculate
\mathcal{A}^{(1)}_{\rm mixed}
, we have to consider all combinations of photons and disperons.
In the case of the box diagram,
\mathcal{A}^{(1)}_{\rm mixed}
is given by

\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -13.92 -13.92 L -10.46 -10.46 L -3.54 -17.38 L 3.38 -10.46 L 10.3 -17.38 L 13.75 -13.92 L 13.92 -13.92}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{gathered}}_{dd}\,.

An analogous split into photon-photon (
pp
), photon-disperon (
pd
), and disperon-disperon (

) contributions is required for the crossed box and the seagull diagram of (
2.2
).
In (
2.2
), we can now use standard techniques to calculate the box diagrams and obtain expression that can be numerically integrated.
However, there is one subtlety.
In the
pd
term of the box (this is not the case for the seagull diagram), we have a singularity
(s-{s_{1}})^{-1}

Since
s
is also above the threshold at
{4m_{\pi}^{2}}
, we integrate over this singularity.
Such a singularity can arise when the energy of the process, in this case
s
, is just enough to produce the intermediate state on-shell.
Hence, it can only happen in
s
-channel processes.
For
t
-channel processes, the corresponding term would be
\propto(t-{s_{1}})^{-1}
and hence unproblematic.
We will come back to the issue of threshold singularities in Section
5
.

3

Implementation into OpenLoops

A key feature of disperon QED is the possibility to generate and compute the required loop amplitudes with an automated numerical tool, such as
OpenLoops

[
64
,
65
]
.
OpenLoops
recursively constructs amplitudes from process-independent building blocks given by the Feynman rules of a model. It provides high numerical stability in one-loop amplitudes due to the on-the-fly reduction of tensor integrals
[
64
]
in combination with a highly efficient numerical stability system
[
65
]
. The
OpenLoops
program provides full QCD and electroweak corrections to the Standard Model, but also allows for the separate calculation of QED and weak corrections with a variable number of massive leptons.

For the applications presented in this paper an extended disperon QED model was implemented in
OpenLoops
, which contains charged pions, massive leptons, massless photons, and disperons. In addition to the standard QED interactions this includes the
sQED
vertices
\pi\,\pi\,\gamma
and
\pi\,\pi\,\gamma\,\gamma
, and analogous vertices with disperons.

The applications described in the previous section require the calculation of three types of one-loop amplitudes in this model, namely amplitudes with no disperon, amplitudes with a single disperon of mass
\sqrt{s_{1}}
in the loop, and amplitudes with two disperons of independent masses
\sqrt{s_{1}}
and
\sqrt{s_{2}}
in the loop.
In addition to the kinematics and physical masses, these amplitudes depend on the values
s_{i}
, which are passed to
OpenLoops
as numerical parameters.

In order to achieve the splitting of amplitudes into sub-contributions as discussed in Section
1
, we use power counting in the pion and electron charge as well as
diagram selection rules for propagator and vertex types, which are implemented in
OpenLoops
.

In
OpenLoops
, divergences are regularised in the ’t Hooft--Veltman scheme
[
72
]
, where external wave functions and momenta are four-dimensional, while loop momenta, metric tensors and Dirac
\gamma
-matrices inside the loops are objects in
d=4-2\epsilon
dimensions.
The numerators of loop integrands are split into a purely four-dimensional part and a
(d-4)
-dimensional remainder. The first is constructed with the
OpenLoops
algorithm while the latter is reconstructed through the insertion of rational
CT

[
73
,
74
]
into tree-level diagrams together with the renormalisation
CT
.
In the on-shell renormalisation scheme, divergent loop amplitudes with a disperon are renormalised differently from the same amplitudes with a massless photon instead. The renormalisation constants that are required for the electron self-energy, mass and QED vertex are given in Appendix
C
. The
UV
(
UV
) pole, however, does not depend on the disperon mass and therefore is the same as in QED. Hence, the rational terms, which depend solely on the
UV
behaviour of the amplitude are also unchanged from QED.

The default regularisation scheme used in
McMule
is the four-dimensional helicity scheme
[
75
,
76
,
77
,
78
]
. Hence, in principle the
OpenLoops
output has to be adapted to be used within
McMule
. However, for our particular one-loop case, the results in the two schemes coincide. The difference between the ’t Hooft--Veltman scheme and the four-dimensional helicity scheme is induced by collinear singularities. Since we deal with QED with massive matter particles, they are not present.

4

Disperon Effective Theory

As we discussed above, we can easily use
OpenLoops
to calculate any required disperon QED amplitude.
However,
{s_{1}}
in the dispersive integral (
1
) can go to infinity.
While these contributions are suppressed by
F({s_{1}})
, which has to become small for
{s_{1}}\to\infty
, and carry an additional
1/{s_{1}}
coming from the dispersion relation, we still want a fast and reliable method to evaluate these parts.
For very large vales of
{s_{1}}
, the disperon must decouple from the theory which means that the amplitude has to be power suppressed by
{s_{1}}
.
By expanding in this regime, we can guarantee a faster and stable evaluation.
To get the desired level of stability, speed and precision, we need to obtain the
1/{s_{1}}
(leading power) and
1/{s^{2}_{1}}
(next-to-leading power) term of the expansion.
This is very similar to the stabilisation technique that
McMule
uses to improve the convergence for real-virtual matrix elements.
There we expand up to next-to-leading power in the soft photon energy using
NTS
stabilisation
[
11
]
.

While it is possible to simply calculate the expanded amplitude using the
MoR
(
MoR
)
[
79
]
, we instead follow a hybrid approach and combine
MoR
with
EFT
-inspired considerations. We will call this approach
DET
(
DET
), even though no attempt is made to rigorously construct an EFT. In particular, for our purpose it is not essential to obtain a complete and non-redundant set of higher-dimensional operators.

As it stands, our method will only work easily at one-loop (which means
NNLO
for
HVP
contributions) but it can be extended to higher loops.

The
MoR
means that to all orders in
{s_{1}}
, the amplitude can be decomposed into a hard contribution and a soft contribution.
In an
EFT
language the hard contribution is a tree-level calculation that uses one-loop Wilson coefficients while the soft contribution uses tree-level Wilson coefficients in a one-loop calculation with effective operators.
To perform any disperon QED calculation we are interested in, we need to find dimension-6 (leading power) and dimension-8 (next-to-leading power) operators for all combinations of leptons, photons, and pions.
The construction of a complete basis, e.g. including the required field redefinitions, beyond dimension 6 is highly non-trivial.
Instead, we propose a hybrid approach where we only use
DET
for the soft contribution while calculating the hard contribution using
MoR
.
This means we will only have to find and match the dimension-6 and dimension-8 operators at tree level.

4.1

Matching at tree level

To perform the matching at tree level we need to consider the diagrams shown in Figure
1
.

Figure 1
:
The three tree-level diagrams that need to be calculated for the matching to
DET
.
The first two diagrams are associated with disperons arising from the
FsQED
approach. In the last one the disperon exclusively interacts with leptons which is due to the
VP
insertion.

We end up with the following Lagrangian

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle\supset C^{[6,\pi\pi]}_{1}\ \bar{\Psi}\gamma^{\mu}\Psi\left[\pi^{\dagger}(-i\overleftarrow{D}_{\mu}-i\overrightarrow{D}_{\mu})\pi\right]

(28)

\displaystyle+C^{[8,\pi\pi]}_{2}\ \left[\bar{\Psi}(i\overleftrightarrow{D}_{\nu})(i\overleftrightarrow{D}^{\nu})\gamma^{\mu}\Psi\right]\left[\pi^{\dagger}(-i\overleftarrow{D}_{\mu}-i\overrightarrow{D}_{\mu})\pi\right]+C^{[8,\pi\pi]}_{3}\ (\bar{\Psi}\gamma^{\mu}\Psi)(\bar{\Psi}\gamma_{\mu}\Psi)\ \pi^{\dagger}\pi

\displaystyle+C^{[6,\Psi\Psi]}_{4}\ (\bar{\Psi}\gamma^{\mu}\Psi)(\bar{\Psi}\gamma_{\mu}\Psi)+C^{[8,\Psi\Psi]}_{5}\ \left[(\bar{\Psi}\gamma_{\mu}i\overleftrightarrow{D}_{\nu}\Psi)\right]\left[(\bar{\Psi}\gamma^{\mu}i\overleftrightarrow{D}^{\nu}\Psi)\right]\,,

where the derivatives only act within the square brackets and
\overrightarrow{D}
acts on the field to the right. The
\pi
and
\Psi
fields contain the creation operator of the negatively charged particle.
The covariant derivative acts as
D_{\mu}=(\partial_{\mu}+ieA_{\mu})
on the
\pi
and
\Psi
fields.
We furthermore have defined the notation
\pi^{\dagger}\overleftarrow{D}_{\mu}\equiv-D_{\mu}\pi^{\dagger}
as well as
\overleftrightarrow{D}_{\mu}=\overleftarrow{D}_{\mu}-D_{\mu}=\overleftarrow{\partial}_{\mu}-\partial_{\mu}
.
The particular form of the operator in the first line of (
28
) was chosen to avoid the necessity of field redefinitions at dimension 8.
The label of the Wilson coefficients indicates the dimension of the corresponding operator and whether it corresponds to the
FsQED
application for disperon QED described in Section
2.2
(
\pi\pi
) or it is used to describe the dispersive treatment of
VP
insertions from Section
2.1
(
\Psi\Psi
).
Because we have used gauge covariant derivatives, these operators do not just create
\ell\ell\pi\pi
or
\ell\ell\ell\ell
vertices but also
\ell\ell\pi\pi\gamma
.
It is easy to show that the Wilson coefficients take the following values

\displaystyle C^{[6,\pi\pi]}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\pi\alpha}{{s_{1}}}\,,\quad C^{[8,\pi\pi]}_{2}=\frac{4\pi\alpha}{{s_{1}}^{2}}\,,\quad C^{[8,\pi\pi]}_{3}=\frac{2(4\pi\alpha)^{2}}{{s_{1}}{s_{2}}}\,,\quad

\displaystyle C^{[6,\Psi\Psi]}_{4}

\displaystyle=-\frac{4\pi\alpha}{{s_{1}}}\,,\quad C^{[8,\Psi\Psi]}_{5}=\frac{4\pi\alpha}{{s^{2}_{1}}}\,.

(29)

For the reader’s convenience, we include the resulting Feynman rules for the
DET
operators in Appendix
D
.
For all explicit results in this work, the additional factors associated to the dispersion relation for each disperon and their integrals are omitted for simplicity and have to be included in the calculation.

We can now use these Feynman rules to perform one-loop calculations for the
VP
and
FsQED
examples discussed here.
Throughout this work, we rely on Package-X
[
67
,
68
]
together with QGraf
[
80
]
.
In the case of
FsQED
, we have to distinguish between the case of one heavy and one (or no) light disperon, and the case of two heavy disperons.
As an example, the soft part of the seagull diagram in
FsQED
with one heavy disperon consists of insertions of contributions at dimension 6 as well as dimension 8 from the operators
O^{[6,\pi\pi]}_{1}
and
O^{[8,\pi\pi]}_{2}
respectively

\displaystyle-\int\frac{{\rm d}{s_{1}}}{{s_{1}}}\frac{\mathrm{Im}\,F_{\pi}^{V}({s_{1}})}{\pi}\hbox to61.86pt{\vbox to35.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-17.67181pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}

}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{}{}{{}}

}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 18.9 -23.62 L 20 -18.86 L 29.53 -21.06 L 31.73 -11.52 L 36.5 -12.62 L 56.69 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

According to the Feynman rules in Appendix
D
, insertions of an operator
O_{j}
are illustrated in Feynman diagrams by a circle around the number
j
.
In the equations above we have illustrated the correct combination of explicit results from (
29
) with the omitted prefactors from the dispersion relation (
21
).

4.2

Hard contributions

Since there are many dimension-8 operators that can be induced at the loop level, we instead
choose to perform the hard calculation in the MoR where all scales except the disperon mass and
the loop momentum are expanded around zero.

In the case of one heavy and one (or no) light disperon, contributions at both dimension 6 and dimension 8 are generated.
At dimension 6, the result of the box diagram cancels exactly with the result of the crossed box, leaving only a
m^{2}_{e}
-suppressed non-zero contribution from the seagull diagram.
At dimension 8, however, all three diagrams contribute.

In the case of two heavy disperons, applying
MoR
generates no contributions at dimension 6.
Due to the cancellation between box and crossed-box diagrams at dimension 8, the only hard contribution in this case arises from the seagull diagram.
For
e^{-}(p_{1})\,e^{+}(p_{2})\to\pi^{-}\,\pi^{+}
, it is given by

\displaystyle\hbox to61.86pt{\vbox to36.14pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-17.67181pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -23.62 L 18.9 -23.62 L 18.9 23.62 L 0 23.62}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\,\Bigg|^{\rm hard}_{\text{dim8}}=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha^{2}m_{e}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\left(\frac{m_{e}^{2}4(d-1)}{d(2+d){s^{2}_{1}}({s_{1}}-{s_{2}})}+\frac{s((d-2){s_{2}}+(6-d){s_{1}})}{2(2+d){s_{1}}({s_{1}}-{s_{2}})^{3}}\right)A_{0}(\sqrt{{s_{1}}})\bar{v}(p_{2})u(p_{1})

\displaystyle+({s_{1}}\leftrightarrow{s_{2}})\,.

(32)

The resulting tadpole integrals, at one loop given by the Passarino Veltman function
A_{0}(m)
, can be easily evaluated.

4.3

Results for DET

To validate our method, we compare
DET
at dimension 6 and dimension 8 as well as
OpenLoops
in
dp
(
dp
) and
qp
(
qp
) against a calculation of the amplitude in Mathematica with arbitrary precision.
To do this, we fix
\sqrt{s}=0.7\,{\rm GeV}
and
t=-0.18\,{\rm GeV}
and vary the disperon masses.
Data and plots can be found at
[
81
]

https://mule-tools.gitlab.io/user-library/pion-pair/cmd/DETstudy.html

Here we consider three scenarios for
ee\to\pi\pi
:
the single-dispersive (
pd
) contribution with a heavy disperon (Figure
2
);
the double-dispersive (
dd
) contribution with one heavy disperon (Figure
3a
);
the double-dispersive contribution with two heavy disperons (Figure
3b
).
We first notice that
OpenLoops
in
qp
is always accurate to at least ten digits which is more than sufficient for our needs.
This also implies that similar tests for
ee\to\pi\pi\gamma
will not require a full calculation in Mathematica for verification.
We also see that
dp
is not always sufficient, especially in the double-dispersive case, for larger values of the dispersion parameter.
Regarding the calculation in
DET
, we notice the power-law scaling in the ratio plots: the slope of the dimension-6 result is much flatter than that of dimension 6 combined with dimension 8 (which includes double insertions of dimension-6 operators).
Furthermore, the double-dispersive contribution with two heavy disperons in Figure
3b
clearly shows the necessity of including dimension-8 effects into
DET
.
In that case, the contribution at dimension 6 is exclusively given by the seagull diagrams that are suppressed by
m^{2}_{e}
.

Our strategy of using
OpenLoops
in
dp
below a cut-off value
s_{\rm cut}
and the
DET
result above this value is therefore validated.
In practice, the value of
s_{\rm cut}
has to be found for every
\sqrt{s}
and is chosen such that the final result is independent of it.
For
\sqrt{s}=0.7\,{\rm GeV}
, we find
s_{\rm cut}=4\,{\rm GeV}^{2}
to be sufficient for Monte Carlo usage.

Finally, we should point out that for large values of the dispersion parameters, the additional suppression of the integrand (
21
) with
1/{s_{1}}
as well as the
VFF
suppression start to matter since
\mathrm{Im}\,F_{\pi}^{V}({s_{1}})\to 0
as
{s_{1}}\to\infty
.
Therefore, we can tolerate larger errors in the regime of large
{s_{1}}
.
Using the
DET
result is beneficial as it is much faster than
OpenLoops
, even for
dp
.

Figure 2
:
Single-dispersive contribution for fixed kinematics but varying the dispersion parameter
s_{1}
, evaluated using the
DET
at dimension 6 and dimension 8 as well as
OpenLoops
in
dp
and
qp
.
The vertical black line represents the value of
{s_{1}}=s
and hence the location of the threshold singularity.
The other jumps are due to zero crossings.
The vertical red line is the value of
s_{\rm cut}
.
Exact refers to the calculation in Mathematica.

(a)

One of the dispersion parameters,
{s_{2}}\sim s,t
, is fixed at a small value and indicated with a vertical black line.
The other parameter is varied.

(b)

Both dispersion parameters are varied with a constant ratio, i.e.
{s_{2}}=2.0\,{s_{1}}
.

Figure 3
:

Double-dispersive contribution for fixed kinematics, similar to Figure
2
.

5

Threshold singularities

We now turn our attention to the threshold singularity we first encountered in (
2.2
).
The problem is that the disperon-photon box in the
pd
contribution for
ee\to\pi\pi
has a
(s-{s_{1}})^{-1}
singularity.
As we are numerically integrating over
{s_{1}}
from the threshold
{4m_{\pi}^{2}}<s
, we will hit this singularity.
This is not a new problem.
Indeed, it was already noted when calculating the
HVP
corrections to
s
-channel Bhabha scattering using what we would now call disperon QED
[
82
]
.
The underlying box diagram is the same, except that it gets multiplied with
R({s_{1}})
from (
3
) rather than
\mathrm{Im}\,F_{\pi}^{V}({s_{1}})
.
In
[
82
]
, the solution was to set
R({s_{1}})\to R({s_{1}})-R(s)
in the numerical integration and correct for this analytically, i.e. they write

where the second integral on the r.h.s is performed analytically with a regulator.
Since for physical applications only the real part of the product of (
5
) and the tree-level amplitude enters, (
5
) works well if the loop integrals are the only source of imaginary parts.
However, in the case of
ee\to\pi\pi
where the complex
VFF
of the Born amplitude contributes further imaginary parts, also the imaginary part of the last integral in (
5
) has to be evaluated.
For the real part, this integral is well defined as the behaviour above and below the
s={s_{1}}
threshold cancels.
Since the box diagram becomes imaginary only for
{s_{1}}<s
, the principal-value prescription no longer cancels the singularity, and a different method must be used.

In
[
53
,
18
]
the real and imaginary parts of
F_{\pi}^{V}({s_{1}})
are treated separately.
The two groups both use a subtraction similar to
[
82
]
and then either use the analytic regulator for the endpoint divergence
[
53
]

or Cutkosky rules
[
83
]
to calculate the imaginary part
[
18
]
.

Here, we opt for an entirely different approach which promises greater universality by constructing a
CT
for the whole amplitude that can be generalised to more complicated topologies.

We will first present our approach in Section
5.1
and calculate the explicit
CT
for
ee\to\pi\pi
in Section
5.2
.
Afterwards, we will discuss its interplay with
IR
(
IR
) divergences in Section
5.3
and conclude in Section
5.4
that an adaption of the CT to more complicated processes is straightforward.

5.1

Landau analysis

To systematise our analysis, we perform a Landau analysis to determine the location of the kinematic singularity on the relevant integral.
For
ee\to\pi\pi
, this is

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{gathered}=\int\frac{[{\rm d}k]}{\big[(k-p_{1}-p_{2})^{2}-s_{1}\big]\big[k^{2}-2k\cdot p_{2}\big]\big[k^{2}\big]\big[k^{2}-2k\cdot p_{4}\big]}\,,

with
p_{1}^{2}=p_{2}^{2}=m_{e}^{2}
,
(p_{1}+p_{2})^{2}=s
,
(p_{2}-p_{4})^{2}=t
and
p_{4}^{2}=m_{\pi}^{2}
.
Employing the Landau equations
[
84
,
85
]
for an
N
-propagator integral

\displaystyle x_{i}\frac{\partial\mathcal{F}}{\partial x_{i}}=0\qquad\forall i\in\{1,...N\}\quad\text{without all }x_{i}=0\,,

(37)

where the
\mathcal{F}
graph polynomial of (
5.1
) is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}=m_{e}^{2}x_{1}(x_{1}+x_{4})+m_{\pi}^{2}x_{4}(x_{1}+x_{4})+(-t)x_{1}x_{4}+{s_{1}}x_{2}(x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}+x_{4})-sx_{2}x_{3}\,,

(38)

does not disclose a singularity.
In general, a more careful analysis is necessary to find all singularities of Feynman integrals, e.g.
[
86
]
.
To that end, we employ the PLD.jl package
[
86
,
87
]
together with PLD-Wrapper
[
88
]
.
This tool enables searches for singularities by allowing for a scaling

\displaystyle x_{i}\to\varepsilon^{w_{i}}x_{i}\quad\text{with}\quad\varepsilon\to 0\,,

(39)

instead of just
x_{i}=0
or
x_{i}\neq 0
as in (
37
).
For the integral in (
5.1
) we find

\displaystyle w=(-1,-1,-1,-3)\quad\text{at}\quad{s_{1}}=s\,.

(40)

Having all
w_{i}\neq 0
means that all propagators pinch simultaneously according to (
39
).
Hence,
{s_{1}}=s
is a singularity of the whole diagram with no contracted propagators (corresponding to setting the corresponding
x_{i}=0
).

Let us now study the integral near the singular point using the
MoR
, i.e. we write
{s_{1}}=s(1-\lambda)
and expand for
\lambda\to 0
.
By utilising automatised tools like asy
[
89
,
90
]
or pySecDec
[
91
]
, we find two region vectors

\displaystyle\vec{v}_{h}=(0,0,0,0)\quad\text{and}\quad\vec{v}_{s}=(-1,-1,-2,-1)\,.

(41)

It is easy to see that the latter is a soft region (with scaling
k\to\lambda\,k
) that starts at
\lambda^{-1-2\epsilon}
while the hard region starts at
\lambda^{0}
.
Since the logarithmically divergent terms are integrable,
the
CT
only needs to capture the
\lambda^{-1-2\epsilon}
singular behaviour, i.e. the soft term.
In momentum space, this can be written as

\displaystyle I_{s}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda^{1+2\epsilon}}\int\frac{[{\rm d}k]}{\big[2k\cdot(p_{1}+p_{2})+s\big]\big[2k\cdot p_{2}\big]\big[k^{2}\big]\big[2k\cdot p_{4}\big]}\equiv\frac{1}{{s_{1}}}\Big(\frac{s}{{s_{1}}-s-i\delta}\Big)^{1+2\epsilon}f(s,t,m_{e},m_{\pi})\,.

(42)

The
MoR
already tells us the dependence on
{s_{1}}-s
which we have included above.
The sign of
i\delta
is due to its appearance in the original integral (
5.1
) as
(\dots-{s_{1}}+i\delta)
.
The additional
1/{s_{1}}
has been factored out to ensure the convergence of the dispersive integral.
The remaining factor
f(s,t,m_{e},m_{\pi})
still needs to be calculated explicitly to construct a
CT
.
While it is possible to calculate this integral directly, it is easier to simply expand the analytic expression for the full box function.
We find

\displaystyle f=

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{\mu^{2}}{s}\Big)^{\epsilon}\frac{\chi_{t}}{\chi_{t}^{2}-1}\Bigg\{\frac{\log(-\chi_{t})}{m_{e}m_{\pi}}\Big(\frac{1}{\epsilon}-2\log(1-\chi_{t}^{2})\Big)

(43)

\displaystyle+\frac{(1+\chi_{e}^{2})(1+\chi^{2}_{\pi})}{s\,\chi_{e}\chi_{\pi}}\bigg[\zeta_{2}-\log(\chi_{e})^{2}-\log(\chi_{\pi})^{2}-\text{Li}_{2}\Big(\chi^{2}_{t}\Big)

\displaystyle-\text{Li}_{2}\Big(1+\chi_{e}\chi_{\pi}\chi_{t}\Big)-\text{Li}_{2}\Big(1+\frac{\chi_{t}\chi_{\pi}}{\chi_{e}}\Big)-\text{Li}_{2}\Big(1+\frac{\chi_{t}}{\chi_{e}\chi_{\pi}}\Big)-\text{Li}_{2}\Big(1+\frac{\chi_{t}\chi_{e}}{\chi_{\pi}}\Big)\bigg]\Bigg\}\,,

where we have introduced the following symbols

\displaystyle\chi_{e}^{2}=\frac{\sqrt{s}-\sqrt{s-4m_{e}^{2}}}{\sqrt{s}+\sqrt{s-4m_{e}^{2}}}\,,\quad\chi_{\pi}^{2}=\frac{\sqrt{s}-\sqrt{s-4m_{\pi}^{2}}}{\sqrt{s}+\sqrt{s-4m_{\pi}^{2}}}\,,\quad\chi_{t}=\frac{1-\sqrt{\frac{(m_{e}+m_{\pi})^{2}-t}{(m_{e}-m_{\pi})^{2}-t}}}{1+\sqrt{\frac{(m_{e}+m_{\pi})^{2}-t}{(m_{e}-m_{\pi})^{2}-t}}}\,.

(44)

The same analysis shows that all other integrals related to this diagram are at most logarithmically divergent.

) a subtraction of the following form

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{\mathrm{Im}\,F_{\pi}(s)}{s}f(s,t,m_{e},m_{\pi})\int_{4m_{\pi}^{2}}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}{s_{1}}}{{s_{1}}}\Big(\frac{s}{{s_{1}}-s-i\delta}\Big)^{1+2\epsilon}\,.

(48b)

While (
48
) will be integrated numerically within the Monte Carlo, the integration of (
48b
) requires our attention.
Note that this integration does not depend on
f
.
We perform a substitution
{s_{1}}\to{4m_{\pi}^{2}}(1+\tau)
which casts the integral into the form

\displaystyle\int_{4m_{\pi}^{2}}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}{s_{1}}}{{s_{1}}}\Big(\frac{s}{{s_{1}}-s-i\delta}\Big)^{1+2\epsilon}=(1-x)^{1+2\epsilon}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}\tau\frac{(x+\tau-i\delta)^{-1-2\epsilon}}{1+\tau}

(49)

with
x=1-s/{4m_{\pi}^{2}}
.
This integral is a well-known hypergeometric function

\displaystyle{}_{2}F_{1}\biggl[\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{a,b}{c};1-\alpha\biggr]=\frac{\Gamma(c)}{\Gamma(b)\Gamma(c-b)}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}\tau\,\tau^{-b+c-1}\ (1+\tau)^{a-c}\ (\tau+\alpha)^{-a}\quad\text{with}\quad\mathrm{Re}\,c>\mathrm{Re}\,b>0\,.

(50)

Using HypExp
[
92
,
93
]
we arrive at

\displaystyle\int_{4m_{\pi}^{2}}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}{s_{1}}}{{s_{1}}}\Big(\frac{s}{{s_{1}}-s}\Big)^{1+2\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(1+2\epsilon)}{\Gamma(2+2\epsilon)}(1-x)^{1+2\epsilon}{}_{2}F_{1}\biggl[\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{1+2\epsilon,1+2\epsilon}{2+2\epsilon};1-x+i\delta\biggr]

(51a)

\displaystyle=(1-x)^{2\epsilon}\Bigg[\log\frac{1}{x-i\delta}+\epsilon\Big(\log^{2}\frac{1}{x-i\delta}-2\text{Li}_{2}(1-x+i\delta)\Big)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})\Bigg]\,.

(51b)

Note that the
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
part of (
51
) is required as it will meet the
1/\epsilon
(
IR
) pole of
f
.
It is now trivial to assemble the integrated
CT
and implement our threshold treatment (
5.2
) in a Monte Carlo.

5.3

Interplay with
IR
divergences

To obtain a physical cross section, the
IR
poles of the virtual amplitude need to cancel against those of the real phase space integration.
In other words, the
IR
structure of
\mathcal{M}_{\rm mixed}^{(1)}=2\,\mathrm{Re}(\mathcal{A}_{\rm mixed}^{(1)}\cdot\mathcal{A}^{(0)*})
needs to match what is predicted by the Yennie-Frautschi-Suura
[
94
]
theorem.

In
McMule
the
IR
structure is handled using
FKSl
subtraction and we will use the same notation as in
[
63
]
for the
IR
pole here.
To be precise, the pole in dimensional regularisation is

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\rm mixed}^{(1)}\Big|_{\text{IR div.}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}-\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\bigg(\frac{1}{\beta_{u}}\log\frac{1+\beta_{u}}{1-\beta_{u}}+\frac{1}{\beta_{t}}\log\frac{1+\beta_{t}}{1-\beta_{t}}\bigg)\mathcal{M}^{(0)}\equiv-\hat{\mathcal{E}}\mathcal{M}^{(0)}\,,

(52)

with the tree-level result
\mathcal{M}^{(0)}
and
\beta_{Q^{2}}=\sqrt{1-4m_{e}^{2}m_{\pi}^{2}/(m_{e}^{2}+m_{\pi}^{2}-Q^{2})^{2}}
.
We will now discuss all
IR
-divergent contributions (
pp
and
pd
;
dd
is finite) and how they interact with each other to demonstrate that (
52
) is still valid in
FsQED
.
A similar discussion using a photon mass regulator can be found in
[
53
,
18
]
where certain sub-leading effects in the regulator need to be considered.
This is not necessary in our scheme.

The simplest
IR
-divergent term is the non-dispersive part

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\rm mixed}^{(1)\,pp}\Big|_{{\text{IR div.}}}=-\frac{\hat{\mathcal{E}}\mathcal{M}^{(0)}}{F_{\pi}(s)}\,.

(53)

It consists of the expected kinematical terms.
One
VFF
of the tree-level result has to be divided out since it is not present in the
pp
amplitude originating from the dispersion relation (
21
).
The structure of the divergence of (
48b
) is given by the
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})
part of the integrated
CT
(
51b
). We find

\displaystyle\big(\eqref{eq:counterterm2}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\rm mixed}^{(1)\,pd}\big)\Big|_{{\text{IR div.}}}

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{\hat{\mathcal{E}}\mathcal{M}^{(0)}}{F_{\pi}(s)}\right)\frac{\mathrm{Im}\,F_{\pi}(s)}{\pi}\log\frac{1}{x}\,.

(54)

In addition to the logarithm, we obtain a form factor mismatch between the tree-level result and the
pd
amplitude.
The most intricate part is the combination of the
pd
amplitude and the threshold
CT
.
Thanks to the subtractive structure of (
5.2
), we arrive at

\displaystyle\left(\eqref{eq:counterterm1}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\rm mixed}^{(1)\,pd}\right)\Big|_{{\text{IR div.}}}

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{\hat{\mathcal{E}}\mathcal{M}^{(0)}}{F_{\pi}(s)}\right)\frac{s}{\pi}\int_{4m_{\pi}^{2}}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}{s_{1}}}{{s_{1}}}\frac{\mathrm{Im}\,F_{\pi}(s)-\mathrm{Im}\,F_{\pi}({s_{1}})}{(s-{s_{1}})}

(55a)

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{\hat{\mathcal{E}}\mathcal{M}^{(0)}}{F_{\pi}(s)}\right)\left(-1+F_{\pi}(s)-\frac{\mathrm{Im}\,F_{\pi}(s)}{\pi}\log\frac{1}{x}\right)\,.

(55b)

The integrand in (
55a
) is a sum of two terms.
The one involving
\mathrm{Im}\,F_{\pi}({s_{1}})
is the l.h.s. of the dispersion relation (
21
) and hence leads to
\propto F_{\pi}(s)
.
The other term, once integrated according to (
51
), will generate the logarithm.
In the end, we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\rm mixed}^{(1)}\Big|_{{\text{IR div.}}}=\mathcal{M}_{\rm mixed}^{(1)\,pp}\Big|_{{\text{IR div.}}}+\mathcal{M}_{\rm mixed}^{(1)\,pd}\Big|_{{\text{IR div.}}}=\eqref{eq:IRdivsqed}+\eqref{eq:IRdivpdsecond}+\eqref{eq:IRdivpdfirst}=-\hat{\mathcal{E}}\mathcal{M}^{(0)}\,.

(56)

Hence, we have established (
52
) as required.
The amplitude containing the corresponding real corrections does not require the disperon QED framework; it is trivial w.r.t. the treatment of the
VFF
.

5.4

Generalising beyond
ee\to\pi\pi

When considering similar topologies for
ee\to\pi\pi\gamma
as shown in Figure
4
, we find that the singularity is at
{s_{1}}=(p_{1}+p_{2}-p_{5})^{2}
.
Singularities arising from box topologies (the first two of Figure
4
) can easily be dealt with through the
CT
for
ee\to\pi\pi
.

Figure 4
:
Topologies contributing to the threshold singularity in
ee\to\pi\pi\gamma
.

When analysing the scaling vector
w_{i}
of the pentagon topology (last topology of Figure
4
)

\displaystyle I=\int\frac{[{\rm d}k]}{\big[(k+p_{1}+p_{2}-p_{5})^{2}-{s_{1}}\big]\big[k^{2}+2k\cdot(p_{2}-p_{5})-2p_{2}\cdot p_{5}\big]\big[k^{2}+2k\cdot p_{4}\big]\big[k^{2}\big]\big[k^{2}+2k\cdot p_{2}\big]}\,,

(57)

we find that it has one zero entry.
This indicates that the singularity arises from a subdiagram.
In order to show this, we find the soft region vector for the first non-zero power in
\lambda

\displaystyle\vec{v}_{s}=(-1,0,-1,-2,-1)\,,

(58)

and expand the integral accordingly.
We find

\displaystyle I_{s}=\int\frac{[{\rm d}k]}{\big[2k\cdot(p_{1}+p_{2}-p_{5})+(p_{1}+p_{2}-p_{5})^{2}\big]\big[-2p_{2}\cdot p_{5}\big]\big[2k\cdot p_{4}\big]\big[k^{2}\big]\big[2k\cdot p_{2}\big]}\,.

(59)

Note that the second propagator now no longer depends on
k
and can therefore be factored out.
Therefore, this integral can be mapped onto the previously calculated
CT
(
42
).
This means that, apart from momentum shifts in (
42
), the
CT
also remains unchanged for the pentagon topology.
A similar result can be obtained by first writing the pentagon integral in terms of boxes using Cayley reduction
[
95
,
96
,
97
]
in which case the mapping to the box integral (
5.1
) becomes easy to see.

The analysis can be repeated for
ee\to\pi\pi\gamma\gamma
and finds the same result: the
CT
that was constructed above is universal.
It can be used to treat threshold singularities in all processes (potentially) covered by the disperon QED framework, such as
\tau\to\pi\pi\nu_{\tau}
described in FsQED
[
98
,
99
]
.

6

Phenomenological results

Additionally to the technical results presented in the previous sections, we will now present some phenomenological results for
ee\to\pi\pi
that validate our method.
We use the following notation to denote a cross section calculated at N
n
LO

\displaystyle\sigma_{n}=\sigma^{(0)}+\sigma^{(1)}+\dots+\sigma^{(n)}\,.

(60)

In
McMule
, the following contributions are available

\displaystyle\sigma^{(1)}

\displaystyle\supset\sigma_{\rm ISC}^{(1)}+\sigma_{\rm mixed\,FsQED}^{(1)}\,,

(61a)

\displaystyle\sigma^{(2)}

\displaystyle\supset\sigma_{\rm ISC}^{(2)}\supset\sigma_{\rm ISC\,VP}^{(2)}\,,

(61b)

where the
VP
contribution at
NNLO
,
\sigma_{\rm ISC\,VP}^{(2)}
, contain only a single
VP
insertion.
We stress that some of these results have been presented before and that most of them are not novel.
Specifically, the mixed
NLO
corrections have been calculated in
FsQED
in
[
53
,
18
]
, using
GVMD
in
[
38
,
18
,
19
]
and using F
\times
sQED in
[
56
,
58
,
18
]
.
We have compared our result for the virtual corrections to
[
53
]
.
The electronic
NNLO
corrections have mostly been presented in
[
7
]
using
McMule
’s calculation of electronic corrections for
e\mu\to e\mu

[
9
]
.
What is new here is that we are now able to resum the
VP
insertion as discussed above.

Specifically, we will consider the process
ee\to\pi\pi
in the CMD-like scenario of
[
7
]
, i.e. with
\sqrt{s}=0.7\,{\rm GeV}
.
We further define

\displaystyle 1\,{\rm rad}

\displaystyle\leq\theta_{\rm avg}=\frac{(\theta^{-}-\theta^{+}+\pi)}{2}\leq\pi-1\,{\rm rad}\,,

(62)

where
\theta^{\pm}
is the angle of the
\pi^{\pm}
.
In addition we require

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\vec{p}_{\pm}|&>0.45\times\sqrt{s}/2\,,\\
\delta\phi&=\big||\phi^{+}-\phi^{-}|-\pi\big|<0.15\,{\rm rad}\,,\\
\xi&=\big|\theta^{+}+\theta^{-}-\pi\big|<0.25\,{\rm rad}\,.\end{split}

(63)

Here we have also used the azimuthal angle
\phi^{\pm}
and three-momenta
\vec{p}_{\pm}
of the
\pi^{\pm}
.
The pion
VFF
is taken from
[
100
]
, however we stress that
McMule
can deal with any
VFF
that is suitable for
FsQED
.

By studying the impact of mixed NLO corrections (
\sigma^{\rm mixed}_{1}=\sigma^{(0)}+\sigma^{(1)}_{\rm ISC}+\sigma_{\rm mixed\,FsQED}^{(1)}
) in
FsQED
, as well as the effect of resummation of the
VP
, we illustrate the size of contributions that are covered by the disperon QED method.
Due to the number of photons coupling to pions, we note that the mixed corrections only contribute to
C
-odd observables such as
\theta^{\pm}
and functions thereof.
As a result, we only show such observables in this work.
All raw data and analysis pipelines can be found at
[
81
]

https://mule-tools.gitlab.io/user-library/pion-pair/cmd/analysis.html

Figures
5
and
6
show the size of different contributions w.r.t.
NLO

ISC
(
\sigma^{\rm ISC}_{1}=\sigma^{(0)}+\sigma^{(1)}_{\rm ISC}
) defined by

\displaystyle\delta[X]=\frac{\sigma^{\rm ISC}_{1}+X}{\sigma^{\rm ISC}_{1}}-1\,,

(64)

where
X\in\{\sigma^{(1)}_{\rm mixed\,FsQED},\,\sigma^{(1)}_{\rm mixed\,F{\times}QED},\,\sigma^{(2)}_{\rm ISC}\}
.
We show the impact of
\sigma^{(1)}_{\rm mixed\,F{\times}QED}
just for illustration.
For completeness, in the bottom panel we also show the size of
\sigma^{\rm ISC}_{1}
compared to
\sigma_{0}

\displaystyle\quad\hat{\delta}[{\sigma^{(1)}_{\rm ISC}}]=\frac{\sigma^{\rm ISC}_{1}}{\sigma_{0}}-1\,,

(65)

as well as the impact on the NNLO prediction of using a resummed
VP
compared to a single
VP
insertion

\displaystyle\tilde{\delta}[\Pi_{\rm res}]=\frac{\sigma_{\rm ISC\,resummed\,VP}^{(2)}}{\sigma_{\rm ISC\,VP}^{(2)}}-1\,.

(66)

As expected for
ISC
, the effect of resummation is small because there cannot be an enhancement by resonances.
Hence, these corrections are standard
NNNLO
(
NNNLO
) terms of the order of
10^{-6}
relative to the
LO
(
LO
).
The
NLO

ISC
corrections described by (
65
) are around
10\%
, as already observed in
[
7
]
.

The
\delta
defined in (
64
) illustrates the importance and symmetry of
NLO
mixed corrections compared to
NNLO

ISC
.
The
NLO
mixed corrections have a strong asymmetry that depends on the pion description used, and exceed the size of the symmetric
NNLO

ISC
in the bulk of the distributions. Only at kinematic boundaries, as e.g. illustrated with
|\cos\theta^{+}|\simeq 0.55
in Figure
6
, the
NNLO
terms are of similar size.

Figure 5
:

The top panel shows the distribution for
\theta_{\rm avg}
defined in (
62
) at
LO
and the best prediction within
McMule
.
The other panels show the various
\delta
’s defined before.
Note that the purple line corresponds to a
NNNLO
effect that results in a
10^{-6}
effect relative to
LO
.

Figure 6
:
Same plot as in Figure
5
but for
\cos\theta^{+}
.

Figure 7
:
Different versions of the forward-backward charge asymmetry defined in (
68
) together with CMD-3 data.

Figure
7
shows the asymmetry as a function of
\sqrt{s}
in the energy range of the CMD-3 experiment, i.e.

\displaystyle A_{\rm FB}(\sqrt{s})=\frac{\sigma(\sqrt{s})|_{\theta_{\rm avg}>\frac{\pi}{2}}-\sigma(\sqrt{s})|_{\theta_{\rm avg}<\frac{\pi}{2}}}{\sigma(\sqrt{s})|_{\theta_{\rm avg}>\frac{\pi}{2}}+\sigma(\sqrt{s})|_{\theta_{\rm avg}<\frac{\pi}{2}}}\,.

(67)

As illustration, the CMD-3 measurements
[
54
,
55
]
are also shown in Figure
7
. This quantity is completely driven by the mixed corrections and the
NNLO

ISC
contribute only to the denominator.
The absence of the
\rho/\phi
interference at
\sqrt{s}\simeq m_{\phi}
in our results is due to its absence in the
VFF
that only considered CMD-2 data
[
100
]
, where this interference was not visible.
When expanding (
67
) in terms of
\alpha
, we can define two versions

\displaystyle A^{\rm expanded}_{\rm FB}(\sqrt{s})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{1}(\sqrt{s})|_{\theta_{\rm avg}>\frac{\pi}{2}}-\sigma_{1}(\sqrt{s})|_{\theta_{\rm avg}<\frac{\pi}{2}}}{\sigma_{0}(\sqrt{s})|_{\theta_{\rm avg}>\frac{\pi}{2}}+\sigma_{0}(\sqrt{s})|_{\theta_{\rm avg}<\frac{\pi}{2}}}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{2})\,,

(68a)

\displaystyle A^{\rm full}_{\rm FB}(\sqrt{s})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{1}(\sqrt{s})|_{\theta_{\rm avg}>\frac{\pi}{2}}-\sigma_{1}(\sqrt{s})|_{\theta_{\rm avg}<\frac{\pi}{2}}}{\sigma_{1}(\sqrt{s})|_{\theta_{\rm avg}>\frac{\pi}{2}}+\sigma_{1}(\sqrt{s})|_{\theta_{\rm avg}<\frac{\pi}{2}}}\,.

(68b)

While the first version,
A^{\rm expanded}_{\rm FB}(\sqrt{s})
, is a strict expansion in
\alpha
, the second version,
A^{\rm full}_{\rm FB}(\sqrt{s})
, uses the
NLO
prediction everywhere.
The difference between these versions is the overall normalisation in the denominator.
As visible in Figure
7
, this does have an impact, in particular for small values of
\sqrt{s}
.
Except for deviations due to different choices of the
VFF
, our result for
A^{\rm full}_{\rm FB}
agrees with
[
18
]
.

7

Conclusion and outlook

We have presented a method, disperon QED, to incorporate data input into a Monte Carlo for loop processes.
It is based on treating the effects of a dispersion relation through a new particle, the disperon, which allows us to rely on automated tools such as
OpenLoops
.
Numerical challenges arising from the dispersive integration are dealt with using an
EFT
-inspired approach,
DET
.
This, combined with a universal threshold subtraction, presents a method with a wide range of applicability.

The method has been applied to the process
ee\to\pi\pi
and implemented in
McMule
. While similar results for this process are already available in the literature, the main thrust of this article is to prepare for applications to more complicated processes.

We plan to apply disperon QED to
ee\to\pi\pi\gamma
, initially considering mixed corrections with photon emission from the electron line.
Adding QED emission to the initial state does not affect the applicability of
FsQED
for the hadronic part.
In fact, it is even conceivable to go one order higher on this part of the amplitudes.
In a second step we will include mixed-correction contributions with photon emission from the final state for
ee\to\pi\pi\gamma
.
Even in this case,
FsQED
is a reasonable starting point. Similarly, form-factor-improved interactions of a photon with protons can be used as a model to describe aspects of lepton-proton scattering. While this requires a slight extension with additional disperon couplings, the core ideas are the same.

Finally, we have considered the resummation of
VP
contributions within loops. These resummation effects are beyond
NNLO
and, hence, typically very small. However, in the vicinity of resonance structures, there can be a substantial enhancement. It will be interesting to investigate these effects for
2\to{2}
scattering in kinematic situations where e.g. the
J/\psi
resonance enters.

With the methods developed here, the technical challenges related to a multitude of phenomenologically relevant computations with generic numerical input in loops can be tackled. Rather than addressing them on a case-by-case base, our approach exploits the availability of general tools and concepts, and provides a versatile tool that renders many computations feasible.

Appendix A

Derivation of the dispersion relation

For completeness we repeat the derivation of the dispersion relation for a function
F(s)
which will help us understand what requirements this function needs to fulfil. Our discussion follows closely
[
101
,
66
,
102
]
.
To start with,

F(s)
shall be analytic in the
s
-plane except for a cut along the positive real axis starting at some finite
s={4m_{\pi}^{2}}
.
This means that Cauchy’s integral formula is applicable

\displaystyle F(s+i\delta)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\mathcal{C}}\frac{F({s_{1}})}{{s_{1}}-s}{\rm d}{s_{1}}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\Bigg(\int_{{4m_{\pi}^{2}}+i\delta}^{R+i\delta}+\int_{\text{large arc}}-\int_{{4m_{\pi}^{2}}-i\delta}^{R-i\delta}+\int_{\text{small arc}}\Bigg)\frac{F({s_{1}})}{{s_{1}}-s}{\rm d}{s_{1}}\,,

(69)

where the integration contour
\mathcal{C}
is defined as shown in Figure
8
and
s
is inside the contour.
Assuming the function is well-behaved around threshold (for example by being linear or constant), the integral over the small arc vanishes as
\delta\to 0
.
Similarly, if
F
falls off fast enough (
{s_{1}}^{-1-\epsilon}
for
\epsilon>0
) as
|{s_{1}}|\to\infty
, the large arc vanishes as well.
Therefore,

\displaystyle F(s)=\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}F(s+i\delta)=\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\Bigg(\int_{{4m_{\pi}^{2}}+i\delta}^{\infty}-\int_{{4m_{\pi}^{2}}-i\delta}^{\infty}\Bigg)\frac{F({s_{1}})}{{s_{1}}-s}{\rm d}{s_{1}}\,.

(70)

We can combine the two integrals

\displaystyle F(s)=\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{4m_{\pi}^{2}}^{\infty}\frac{F({s_{1}}+i\delta)-F({s_{1}}-i\delta)}{{s_{1}}-s-i\delta}{\rm d}{s_{1}}=\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{4m_{\pi}^{2}}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{Im}\,F({s_{1}})}{{s_{1}}-s-i\delta}{\rm d}{s_{1}}\,.

(71)

In the last step we have used the Schwarz reflection principle

\displaystyle F(s^{*})=F^{*}(s)\quad\forall\,s\,\text{except the cut}\,,

(72)

which requires
F(s)
to be real for real-valued
s
below the threshold.
Hence,
F({s_{1}}+i\delta)-F({s_{1}}-i\delta)=2i\,\mathrm{Im}\,F({s_{1}})
for
\delta\to 0
.
However, the functions we consider often do not fall off quickly enough, i.e.
F(s)
does not scale like
s^{-1-\epsilon}
.
Instead, we use a (once) subtracted dispersion relation and decompose
F(s)\to(F(s)-F(0))/s

\displaystyle\frac{F(s)-F(0)}{s}=\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{4m_{\pi}^{2}}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{Im}\,F({s_{1}})-\mathrm{Im}\,F(0)}{{s_{1}}-s-i\delta}\frac{{\rm d}{s_{1}}}{{s_{1}}}\,.

(73)

Often, we have
F(0)=0
(e.g. in the case of
VP
corrections) or
F(0)=1
(in the case of form factors).
Under this assumption we can further simplify

\displaystyle\frac{F(s)-F(0)}{s}=-\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{4m_{\pi}^{2}}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{Im}\,F({s_{1}})}{s-{s_{1}}+i\delta}\frac{{\rm d}{s_{1}}}{{s_{1}}}\,.

(74)

In summary, the (once subtracted) dispersion relation requires that
F(s)
is analytic except for
s>{4m_{\pi}^{2}}
along the real axis, is real for real-valued
s
below the threshold, falls off faster than
F\sim s^{-\epsilon}
for any
\epsilon>0
, and is well-behaved around the threshold.

Figure 8
:
The contour
\mathcal{C}
for the dispersion relation.

Appendix B

Compton tensor

In this appendix we will briefly summarise the background of
FsQED
and
FxsQED
.
Our discussion will follow
[
29
,
103
,
30
]
and its aim is to clarify the notation and the relationship between
FsQED
and
FxsQED
.

In (
2.2
) we have stated the Feynman rule for the process
\gamma^{*}(k)\to\pi^{+}(p)\pi^{-}(q)
as a
sQED
Feynman rule multiplied with a form factor, i.e.
FxsQED
.

) can be carried out using the same Feynman rule.

The full tensor is a complicated, non-perturbative object.
However, it can be written in terms of five form factors that can in principle be measured or estimated
[
29
,
103
,
30
]
.
Alternatively, we can derive a dispersive decomposition of
W^{\mu\nu}
.
To do this, one fixes e.g.
s=(p+q)^{2}
and considers the discontinuities
D_{i}
along the
t=(k_{1}-p)^{2}
and
u=(k_{2}-p)^{2}
cuts

\displaystyle W^{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}t^{\prime}\frac{D_{t}^{\mu\nu}(t^{\prime},s)}{t^{\prime}-t}+\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}u^{\prime}\frac{D_{u}^{\mu\nu}(u^{\prime},s)}{u^{\prime}-u}\,.

(76)

Using unitarity relations, the discontinuities can be written as a sum over all possible intermediary states
|n\rangle
.
Schematically,

\displaystyle D_{t}(t,s)\sim\sum_{n}\int{\rm d}\Phi_{n}\langle\pi^{-}(q)\gamma^{*}(-k_{2})|n\rangle\langle n|\pi^{-}(-p)\gamma^{*}(k_{1})\rangle\,.

(77)

The first state in this sum is the pion itself,
|n\rangle=|\pi\rangle
, which results in (the dispersive cut is indicated with the black line crossing the pion propagator)

\displaystyle D_{t}^{\mu\nu}(t,s)

In other words, it is possible to obtain the pion-pole contribution (
|n\rangle=|\pi\rangle
) for
\gamma^{*}\gamma^{*}\to\pi\pi
by calculating in
sQED
and multiplying with the
VFF
, in other words
FxsQED
.

Note that the pion-pole approximation used here assumes that the propagator of the intermediary particle still remains that of a pion.
This approximation has been shown to be good enough for
\gamma^{*}\gamma^{*}\to\pi\pi
at low energies.
For
\gamma^{*}\gamma^{*}\gamma\to\pi\pi
, it is less clear whether
FxsQED
is a good approximation.
A first step has been taken by constructing the dispersive decomposition of this new Compton tensor in the limit where the on-shell photon is soft
[
33
]
.
This has indicated that
FxsQED
for the Compton tensor is a well-defined reasonable starting point which was also indicated via the so-called Pisa Consensus of the RadioMonteCarLow 2 Group
[
104
]
.

Beyond pions, a similar construction is possible for
\gamma^{*}\gamma^{*}\to pp
and
\gamma^{*}p\to\gamma^{*}p
as entering
\ell p\to\ell p
scattering.
However, the equivalent relation of (
B
) for a spinor instead of a scalar no longer holds:
the proton-pole contribution no longer agrees with the Born amplitudes that can be calculated using the equivalent of
FxsQED
where we use a Feynman rule that involves form factors (now two rather than just a single
VFF
)

with two tensor functions
T_{i}^{\mu\nu}
that can be reconstructed from
[
105
,
106
]

\displaystyle T_{1}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{M}g^{\mu\nu}\,,

(89)

\displaystyle T_{2}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4iM^{3}}\Big(-i\ k_{1}^{\mu}k_{2}^{\nu}+k_{1}\cdot(p+q)\ \sigma^{\mu\nu}+\gamma^{5}\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}k_{2\alpha}k_{1\beta}\Big)\,.

(90)

For a discussion on the difference between Born and pole contributions, see for example
[
107
,
108
,
109
,
110
]
.
In the case of forward scattering (
(p-k_{1})^{2}\sim 0
), we also refer to the review in
[
111
]
.

Both for pions and protons, contributions beyond the pion or proton pole are delicate.
In the pion case, the intermediary vector-meson states need to be unitarised to get the
f_{2}(1270)
resonance correct which destroys the simple
FxsQED
picture for the Compton tensor
[
30
]
.
For the proton case the pole approximation for the
\Delta(1232)
intermediate state has been shown to be insufficient
[
112
]
.

Appendix C

Renormalisation

Including a disperon of mass
\sqrt{{s_{1}}}
does add additional terms to the QED renormalisation constants.
In total, the vertex and mass renormalisation read

\displaystyle\delta Z^{(1)}_{2}(\sqrt{{s_{1}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{2\pi}\left(\frac{\mu^{2}}{m^{2}}\right)^{\epsilon}

\displaystyle\left[-2-\frac{3}{2\epsilon}\right.

\displaystyle-\frac{3\left(4m^{4}+2m^{2}{s_{1}}-{s^{2}_{1}}\right)}{2m^{2}(4m^{2}-{s_{1}})}\,\text{DiscB}(m^{2},m,\sqrt{{s_{1}}})

(91a)

\displaystyle+\left.\left(1-\frac{3{s^{2}_{1}}}{4m^{4}}\right)\log\left(\frac{m^{2}}{{s_{1}}}\right)-2-\frac{1}{2\epsilon}-\frac{3{s_{1}}}{2m^{2}}\right]\,,

\displaystyle\delta Z^{(1)}_{m}(\sqrt{{s_{1}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{2\pi}\left(\frac{\mu^{2}}{m^{2}}\right)^{\epsilon}

\displaystyle\left[-2-\frac{3}{2\epsilon}\right.

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\left(4+\frac{2{s_{1}}}{m^{2}}\right)\text{DiscB}(m^{2},m,\sqrt{{s_{1}}})

(91b)

\displaystyle-\left.\frac{{s^{2}_{1}}}{4m^{4}}\log\left(\frac{m^{2}}{{s_{1}}}\right)-\frac{1}{4}\left(8+\frac{6}{\epsilon}+\frac{2{s_{1}}}{m^{2}}\right)\right]\,,

where we have used from Package-X

\displaystyle\text{DiscB}(m^{2},m,\sqrt{{s_{1}}})=\frac{\sqrt{{s_{1}}\left({s_{1}}-4m^{2}\right)}}{m^{2}}\log\left(\frac{{s_{1}}+\sqrt{{s_{1}}\left({s_{1}}-4m^{2}\right)}}{2m\sqrt{{s_{1}}}}\right)\,.

(92)

The first lines of (
91
) contain the pure QED contribution.
Note that (
91
) are given in the ’t Hooft--Veltman scheme.
As discussed in Section
3
, they need to be combined with the rational
CT
before being passed to
OpenLoops
.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\quad=\quad 2ie\,(p_{1}+p_{2})^{2}\,(\gamma_{\mu})\,,

(94)

3

\displaystyle=\quad i\,(\gamma_{\mu})[\gamma^{\mu}]\,,

(95)

as well as for
e^{-}(p_{1})e^{+}(p_{2})\to\ell^{-}(q_{1})\ell^{+}(q_{2})

4

\displaystyle=\quad i\,(\gamma_{\mu})[\gamma^{\mu}]\,,

(96)

5

\displaystyle=\quad-i\,(p_{1}+p_{2})^{2}(\gamma_{\mu})[\gamma^{\mu}]\,.

(97)

This process specification means that the momenta
p_{i}
are considered as incoming, the momenta
q_{i}
as outgoing.
Additionally, the Wilson coefficients
C_{i}
have to be included in the Feynman rule.
This was for example done in (
4.1
).
